1 Introduction
  CARTA exemplifies the efficiency challenges that transit agencies face throughout the U.S., espe-
cially in mid-sized southern cities, where agencies have to balance the tension between improving
service coverage and improving ridership. CARTA spends more than $1.1 million annually on
fuel while supporting several different transportation modalities, including a fixed-route service,
demand-response service (using neighborhood shuttles), and paratransit service. Through these
three services, CARTA provides over 3 million passenger trips per year. The goal of the project is
to integrate the operations to gain efficiency. For example, paratransit operations account for 22%
of total service miles but less than 4% of passenger trips.
  We hypothesize that there is a significant opportunity for lowering the overall energy cost if
we focus on systemwide integration, which co-designs the schedules and services of fixed-route
transit, microtransit, and paratransit. In particular, we observe the transit agencies can solve these
problems at three levels (a) deciding the area and frequency of fixed-line trips; (b) deciding the
areas and services that can be served through integrated paratransit and microtransit operations;
(c) optimizing the vehicles that are used for each trip, considering that the advantage of Electrical
Vehicles (EVs) over Internal Combustion Engine Vehicles (ICEVs) varies depending on the route
and time of day (e.g., the benefit of EVs is higher in slower traffic with frequent stops and lower on
highways), the assignment can have a significant effect on energy use and, hence, environmental
impact; and finally, (d) working to optimize the charging schedules and charger placements to
reduce the overall impact on electrical grid.
  Developing scalable algorithms for real-time fleet management and operational optimization is
a challenging problem. Specifically, it requires solving scheduling problems that are variants of
the classical Vehicle Routing Problem (VRP) and the related Dial-a-Ride Problem, which are NP-
hard and typically solved using heuristic methods. Further, the problem solution poses a big data
challenge due to scale and heterogeneity.
  While there is a long and rich literature of heuristic techniques for solving VRP problems, scala-
bility has been an issue even for heuristic methods. Typically, scalability is handled by decompos-
ing the VRP problem into a trip generation phase, which creates candidate trips, and an assignment
phase, assigning the fleet to a subset of the candidate trips. While this decomposition leads to in-
creased scalability, these two sub-problems are NP-hard and require algorithmic sophistication.
Further, decisions must be made facing uncertainty (e.g., future demand requests and traffic con-
ditions). Despite these uncertainties, transit services must meet strict requirements: paratransit
requests must be served within a limited time, fixed-route schedules must be closely followed, and
many of these decisions must be made in real-time. In this project, we are designing decision-
theoretic algorithms that solve the aforementioned challenges.
  The project is divided into three phases. In the first phase, we focused on the paratransit services
as it provides a great opportunity for optimization.1 Paratransit services are provided by transit
agencies as a supplement to fixed-route services to ensure equity for disabled people who can-
not use fixed-route transportation. However, the demand varies significantly over the course of
a week (Fig. 1b). One of the challenges in handling paratransit operations is the Federal Transit
Administration guideline that limits the maximum wait times for passengers. Without adequate
 1
 In prior work [1], the team has already developed algorithms for optimally (minimizing total energy impact)
assigning vehicles of different types to fixed-route trips given predicted traffic and weather conditions and considering
road elevation changes.

optimization procedures, transit agencies add substantial slack to their schedules and limit trips to
only a few passengers each, which leads to low energy efficiency. We can quantify this loss using
deadhead miles, which are miles driven by paratransit vehicles without any passengers (Fig. 1c).

Our tasks in Phase I and Phase II are summarized below.

 1. Develop the paratransit optimization algorithms that can reduce the slack in the system and
 make the system more efficient.
 2. Design and develop camera based algorithms that can provide accurate information about
 occupancy levels in the vehicles.
 3. Develop a simulation system which can be used to simulate the impact of transit policies and
 charging times on the stability of the grid.
 4. Integrate the para-transit application into an online web application and develop the neces-
 sary application programming interfaces.
 5. Demonstrate that the efficiency improvement of the designed algorithms is greater than 5%.

2.1 Paratransit optimization algorithms
  Our current progress includes three parts of the online integrated optimizer that arranges the
paratransit dispatch trip roster: online booking, day-ahead scheduling, and real-time requests. The
offline vehicle routing problem with online bookings is a reinforcement-learning inspired technique
that provides the ability to create coarse solutions as each request from a client arrives on the phone
(at least a day before). The technique uses a simulated-annealing method as the any-time algorithm
to reduce the size and complexity of the action space [2]. Eventually, the solutions are refined using
a day ahead scheduling algorithm [3] that builds upon prior work by Co-PI Samaranayake from
Cornell University [4]. The critical insight is that we can use an online solver in the offline mode
and avoid the complexity of computing the solution for the whole day in one shot and considering
smaller time windows in a rolling horizon fashion. Finally, the real-time requests on the day
of travel are addressed using a Monte Carlo tree search approach [5]. The advantage of online
scheduling is the ability to handle uncertainties and complex constraints. Our initial results are
promising and show that the heuristics can handle large vehicle capacities and provide results
within a few seconds. We describe the key algorithms below.
2.1.1 Offline Vehicle Routing Problem with Online Bookings
  Vehicle routing problems (VRPs) can be divided into two major categories: offline VRPs, which
consider a given set of trip requests to be served, and online VRPs, which consider requests as they
arrive in real-time. During the research, we identified a real-world problem that is not addressed

 Page 3

                                               Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025
Reduction in Cost 60 %

 40 %

 20 %

 0%

 −20 %
 2 hours 3 hour 4 hour
 Broad Window Size

Figure 2: Reduction in total cost due to using our approach for selecting tight pickup windows supported by anytime
algorithm, compared to using naı̈ve pickup windows with VROOM (■) and Google OR-Tools (■) as offline VRP
solvers.

by existing formulations. When passengers book trips over the phone, they often request broad
pickup windows (e.g., going for groceries in the afternoon, sometime between 2pm and 5pm).
While passengers may have no preference between pickup times within these broad windows,
they do strongly prefer to know in advance when they will be picked up. So, transit agencies
must confirm a tight pickup window (e.g., 30 minute interval within the broad window) at the
time of booking. The reason for this is very practical: vehicles may arrive at any time within the
confirmed windows, and passengers need to be ready to be picked up. This presents an interesting
online optimization problem: how to select tight pickup windows at the time of booking, based on
information available at the time, assuming that vehicle routes will be optimized as an offline VRP
once all the trips have been booked? To address this gap between offline and online problems,
we introduce a novel formulation, the offline vehicle routing problem with online bookings. This
problem is very challenging computationally since it faces the complexity of considering large
sets of requests—similar to offline VRPs—but must abide by strict constraints on running time—
similar to online VRPs.
 We formulate this as the offline vehicle routing problem with online bookings. We assume that
trip requests with broad pickup windows are received one-by-one, and for each request, a tight
pickup window must be selected in a matter of seconds. At the end of the booking process, vehi-
cle routes are optimized as an offline VRP based on the selected tight windows.The objective of
optimizing the tight pickup windows is to minimize the cost of the resulting offline VRP. Our so-
lution approach combines an anytime algorithm with a reinforcement-learning based policy. The
anytime algorithm is executed between consecutive requests and provides supporting input for the
policy, taking advantage of the extra time between requests (e.g., there may be 5 minutes between
consecutive calls, but only a few seconds to respond during a call). The policy is responsible for
selecting tight pickup windows, taking advantage of the input received from the anytime algorithm.
The advantage of a learning based policy is that its computational cost is very low once it has been
trained on historical data.
 Next, we demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed approach by showing that optimizing
tight pickup windows can lead to significant reductions in cost. Since the online bookings problem
is novel to the best of our knowledge, existing VRP solvers do not address the online selection
of tight pickup windows. Therefore, to provide baselines for comparison, we consider existing
offline VRP solvers with “naı̈vely selected” pickup windows, which we define as selecting the
middle interval of broad pickup windows as tight windows.
 Fig. 2 shows the reduction in the total cost of vehicle routes due to using our proposed approach,

 Page 4

                               Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025

compared to using VROOM and Google OR-Tools with naı̈vely selected pickup windows. For each
comparison, we evaluate the algorithms on 180 days of paratransit data, and plot the distributions.
We observe a significant reduction in costs compared to both baseline solvers. Further, we find that
our approach is robust to variations in the duration of broad pickup windows since it maintains a
significant advantage when broad windows are 2- or 4-hours long, even though the decision policy
was trained only on 3-hour broad windows.
 This approach was published in the following paper: Amutheezan Sivagnanam et al. “Offline
Vehicle Routing Problem with Online Bookings: A Novel Problem Formulation with Applications
to Paratransit”. In: 31st International Joint Conference on Artificial Intelligence (IJCAI). July
2022.
2.1.2 Offline Paratransit Vehicle Scheduling via Rolling Horizon Optimization
 While the previous approach described above was focused on optimized and changing the book-
ing windows to be more optimal, the second algorithm we have developed focuses on the day-ahead
optimization once the booking windows have been finalized. Our approach uses a rolling horizon
framework that provides a better trade-off between computational efficiency and solution qual-
ity. In particular, our approach uses an online optimization technique that decomposes the fixed
time horizon and solves a sequence of much smaller optimization problems. The benefit of such
an approach is tractability and fast computation. However, using small batch sizes has an innate
limitation of being myopic because it makes greedy vehicle dispatching decisions with restricted
information in each batch. Therefore, in our approach we use a a rolling horizon framework that
considers a sliding window of larger time intervals and utilizes more information (from the offline
problem instance), while still limiting the computational cost in each batch. Note that for each
batch we utilize our prior work [4].

 Table 3: Overview of Datasets

 NYC NYC
 CARTA
 Scenario 1 Scenario 2
 Number of Days 30 31 31
 Mean Requests Per Day 172 129 2587
 Std. Dev. Requests Per Day 33 29 570

  We tested our algorithm on two datasets as shown in Table 3. First, we used paratransit trip
requests data provided by a public transit agency. Second, we used New York City taxi data to
investigate the scalability of our approach on a larger dataset. In particular, we randomly sampled
20% of the NYC requests with an average of 2,587 requested trips per day.
  Our comparison is against two baselines. The first baseline is a fully online solver where we
we use our own approach with a rolling horizon factor of 0 to represent the online solver. By
setting the rolling horizon factor to 0, our solver is simplified to a purely online approach as it
does not utilize future knowledge. We refer to this baseline as RH0 comparing to our approach
RH1, RH2, RH3, which used rolling horizon factor of 1, 2, 3, respectively. Note we tried the
problem with a a heuristic using guided local search (GLS) [6] implemented in Google OR-Tools,
the commonly used open source VRP solver [7] as well. However, the scale of the problem with
a limited maximum computation time for a given day of one second per request was unable to be
solved by the Google OR tool.

Figure 3: (a) Service rate evaluated on the CARTA paratransit dataset. (b) Service rate evaluated on NYC dataset. (c)
Computation time per request evaluated on the CARTA dataset. (d) Compute time, NYC dataset.

  The service rate per day for the 30 experimental days is provided in Fig. 3a. Overall, our frame-
work (RH1, RH2, RH3) outperforms the online approach (RH0). In an experiment with 7 fleets,
RH3 brings an increase in service rate from 84.2% to 92.2% compared to RH0, which corresponds
to a 9.5% increase. We observe that the slope of the service rate is reduced as the rolling hori-
zon factor increases. This is because future information has a more significant impact on current
decisions when the events are imminent. However, computational time exponentially increases
as we increase the rolling horizon factor as in Fig. 3c. In practice, operators can decide a proper
look-ahead time by trading off between desirable service rate and allowable computation time.
  The results of the scalability test with NYC scenario highlight the scalability of our framework.
The number of requests per day is significantly larger than the CARTA use case. While the GLS
could not get any feasible solution within the given time limit, our framework achieves the solution
within 1 second. In the rolling horizon framework, the increases in look ahead time are restricted,
because the computation time explodes as the rolling horizon factor increases. We could not get
the result of RH3 within the time limit as in Fig. 3d, thus the reasonable rolling horizon factor in
this setting would be 2. Likewise, in practice, operators can decide a proper look-ahead time by
considering how much time they can spend for computation. The service rate achieved is shown
in Fig. 3b.
  These results are presented in a paper that is currently under review: Youngseo Kim et al. “Offline
Paratransit Vehicle Scheduling via Rolling Horizon Optimization”. In: 25 International Confer-
ence on Intelligent Transportation Systems, ITSC 2022 (under review). 2022.
2.1.3 Online Scheduling Algorithm
  While the day-ahead algorithms discussed in the previous sections provide the schedule of trips,
transit agencies also have to respond to trip requests that arrive in real-time, which entails solving

 Page 6

                                 Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025

Figure 4: An overview of MC-VRP. A decision epoch corresponds with a new trip request Rt . We generate request-
vehicle (RV) and vehicle-vehicle (VV) graphs by combining a heuristic based pickup and delivery problem with
time-windows (PDPTW) solver with metrics to quickly estimate the utility of route plans. The PDPTW is NP-hard [8,
9]; as a result, we use a heuristic subroutine to solve it. We then select promising actions from the graphs by sampling
independent sets of high weights to be evaluated by an online approach based on MCTS.

hard combinatorial and sequential decision-making problems under uncertainty. To avoid deci-
sions that lead to significant inefficiency in the long term, vehicles should be allocated to requests
by optimizing a non-myopic utility function or by batching requests together and optimizing a my-
opic utility function. While the former approach is typically offline, the latter can be performed
online. We point out two major issues with such approaches when applied to paratransit services
in practice. First, it is difficult to batch paratransit requests together as they are temporally sparse.
Second, the environment in which transit agencies operate changes dynamically (e.g., traffic con-
ditions can change over time), causing the estimates that are learned offline to become stale.
  To address these challenges, we developed a fully online approach to solve the dynamic vehicle
routing problem (DVRP) with time windows and stochastic trip requests that is robust to changing
environmental dynamics by construction as it performs planning online. Our approach is called
MC-VRP (Monte Carlo tree search based solution for vehicle routing problem). We model the
DVRP as a route-based Markov decision process (MDP) [10]. Given an arbitrary state of the MDP,
we use generative models over customer requests and travel time to simulate the environment under
consideration, which in turn enables us to use Monte Carlo tree search [11] to find promising
actions for the state. Our approach does not require offline training, the only requirement is a
generative model that can be sampled at run-time. To tackle the intractably large action space, we
leverage the structure of the problem to find promising actions. Specifically, given a set of routes
and a new request, we create a weighted graph based on a budget-based heuristic whose edges
represent: (a) which vehicles can serve the new request, and (b) which vehicles can swap unpicked
requests from their routes to maximize utility. Then, we sample independent sets from the graph
that correspond to feasible actions for the given state of the MDP.
  We provide an overview of MC-VRP in Fig. 4. Our approach consists of the following three
broad components: 1) For a given state of the MDP, we sample feasible and promising actions by
exploiting the structure of the problem. To compute feasibility, we use a heuristic-based solution
approach to the PDPTW. To compute the potential utility of a feasible action, we introduce two
heuristics, one based on passenger travel times and another based on budget (or slack) in route
plans. 2) We compute weighted graphs based on the feasible actions and their potential utilities.
Specifically, we create vehicle-vehicle (VV) and request-vehicle (RV) graphs (we describe the

Figure 6: (a) Computation time per request in seconds for each day in the test set for 3, 4 and 5 vehicles. Median
computation time per request for MC-VRP (budget) is 38 seconds, 49 seconds and 40 seconds for 3, 4 and 5 vehicles
respectively. DRLSA, MA-RTV and greedy all had median computation times less than 3 seconds per request. (b)
Runtime analysis: Percent change in service rate for MC-VRP (budget) with a cutoff of 30 seconds per request
compared to no cutoff. For three vehicles there was a median of 1.2 percent decrease in service rate on test set while
the computation cutoff had negligible affect for four and five vehicles. (c) Robustness to roadway congestion: Percent
change in service rate for DRLSA, MA-RTV, greedy-budget and MC-VRP (budget) on test set using congested travel

5 vehicle fleets compared to the baselines.

graphs below). Then, we generate promising actions by sampling independent sets from the graphs
(based on the weights of the sets). 3) The sampled actions are then used by our online non-myopic
planner based on Monte Carlo tree search. To build the search tree into the future, we sample
future requests from a data-driven generative model. Finally, to lower computation time, we utilize
pre-computed samples of requests and root parallelization [12] to efficiently explore the search
space and recommend an action for the given state.
  We evaluate the performance of MC-VRP against two greedy approaches: greedy-PTT, greedy-
budget. We use six months of paratransit trip requests between January 1, 2021 and July 1, 2021
from CARTA. Our dataset consists of a total of 25,843 trip requests. We randomly selected 15
days from the dataset for the evaluation (test) set. The remaining 114 days were use to compute
100 synthetic days worth of trip requests (i.e., chains) for the generative model required by our
approach as it looks into the future during planning.
  Greedy assignment consists of first generating feasible actions and then selecting the action with
the highest utility. For comparison, we include greedy assignment using both the passenger travel
time (PTT) and the utility that maximizes the budget (or slack) in route plans. We also compare

 Page 8

                               Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025

our approach to two myopic online approaches (greedy and MA-RTV) and one non-myopic hybrid
approach called Deep reinforcement learning-based vehicle routing [13] (DRLSA). The MA-RTV
stands for Myopic and Anytime trip-vehicle assignment-based on RTV graphs [4]. It is the baseline
standard developed by Co-PI Samarnayake and is known to be the state of the art algorithm that
batches requests together and then maximizes the myopic utility for the specific batch of requests.
For each batch, the algorithm creates an RTV graph by checking shareability between requests
as well as requests and vehicles. Since the best passengers-vehicle pair is found in each batch,
the approach works well in practice despite being myopic (our experiments confirm this). For
all baselines, we use the same number of vehicles (we present results by varying the number of
vehicles) and vehicle capacity as MC-VRP.
 Our primary objective is to maximize the number of requests serviced each day. The service rate
per day for the 15 days in the evaluation (test) set is provided in Fig. 5 with fleet sizes of 3, 4, and
5 vehicles. We first present results in a setting where MC-VRP (budget) and MC-VRP (PTT) are
allowed to run for 1000 iterations without early termination. First, we note that 5 vehicles is enough
to service all of the requests on most days as MC-VRP (budget), MC-VRP (PTT), and MA-RTV all
have a median service rate of 100%. We observe that MC-VRP (budget) outperforms all baselines
for fleet sizes of 3 and 4 with with a median service rate of 87.0% and 97.6% respectively. MC-
VRP (PTT) had a service rate of 84.7% and MA-RTV had a service rate of 81.7% for 3 vehicles.
MA-RTV outperformed MC-VRP (PTT) in the case of 4 vehicles with a median service rate of
96.2% compared to 95.8%.
 We also observe that the budget-based heuristic works better than the travel time-based heuristic
for both Monte Carlo tree search as well as greedy assignment. Indeed, MC-VRP (budget) results
in a higher service rate than MC-VRP (PTT) and greedy-budget outperforms greedy-PTT for all
fleet sizes. This indicates that the budget utility, which aims to maximize time for which a vehicle
has no passengers on-board, can be used to quickly compute promising actions to explore. It
is important to note that while we focus on paratransit services, the budget-based heuristic can be
applied for other DVRPs with capacity and time window constraints. We also observe that DRLSA
had the lowest service rate across all fleet sizes. An influencing factor, is that time windows are
soft constraints in DRSLA which is not particularly suited to paratransit settings.
 The computation time per request for MC-VRP and the baselines is shown in Fig. 6a. MC-VRP
(budget) takes slightly longer than MC-VRP (PTT) to compute a decision, with a median compu-
tation time of 38 seconds, 49 seconds, and 40 seconds as compared to 36 seconds, 44 seconds, and
37 seconds for 3, 4, and 5 vehicles respectively. DRLSA, MA-RTV, and greedy all had median
computation times less than 3 seconds. In this context, the fact that MC-VRP is both non-myopic
and fully online is reflected in the higher runtimes; while the observed runtimes are acceptable in
our setting, i.e., paratransit services, the application of online and non-myopic methods remains an
open question in general VRP settings. Note that if the MCTS evaluation of our approach is not
used (i.e., the early stopping time approaches 0), our approach simplifies to the greedy baseline,
which takes less than a second on average. This observation means that the majority of computa-
tion time in our approach is spent on MCTS.
 Being an anytime algorithm, the MCTS evaluation can be run in the background between calls.
In such a setting, the algorithm is stopped early (in our case, before the default number of iterations
is reached), but outputs a feasible solution. In our dataset, 99% of requests arrive more than 30
seconds after the previous request and the median time between requests was approximately 5
minutes. Therefore, we evaluate the performance of MC-VRP with a 30 second cut-off time in

 Page 9

                               Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025

Fig. 6b. We observe only a negligible decrease in service rates; the median service rate decreases
by 1.2% for 3 vehicles and 0% for 4 and 5 vehicles.
  To evaluate the robustness of the proposed approach with respect to changing environmental
conditions, we change the travel time distribution in the city. We evaluated DRLSA, MA-RTV,
greedy-budget and MC-VRP (budget) on the 15 day evaluation set using the congested travel time
network and compared the resulting service rates to those generated without congestion. We ob-
serve that MC-VRP (budget) incurs less of a decrease in service rate compared to DRLSA, MA-
RTV and greedy-budget across all sizes of vehicle fleets as shown in Fig. 6c. As expected, DRLSA
had the greatest decreases in service rate due to its reliance on a value function that was trained
offline using the free-flow travel time matrix. While hybrid approaches such as DRLSA have ac-
cess to the real-time travel conditions, it is difficult to re-train offline components, resulting in a
higher degradation in performance. Both MA-RTV and greedy-budget outperform DRLSA when
evaluated for robustness. This result is expected since MA-RTV and greedy-budget are both online
approaches, it is able to adapt to the updated matrix. We also observe that MC-VRP (budget) per-
forms better MA-RTV and greedy-budget, showing that our non-myopic online solution improved
upon the myopic online approaches as well.
  This paper is published in the proceedings of the ICCPS 2022 conference: Michael Wilbur et al.
“An Online Approach to Solve the Dynamic Vehicle Routing Problem with Stochastic Trip Re-
quests for Paratransit Services”. In: 13th ACM/IEEE International Conference on Cyber-Physical
Systems (ICCPS). May 2022.
2.2 Simulation Environment
  We have completed the initial work on a transportation and power grid simulation during Phase I.
The simulator can help evaluate the impact of different transit trips and subsequent vehicle choices
and charging schedules. The core component of this simulator is he transit-gym which is described
next. This is then integrated with the GridLab-D based power grid simulator.
2.2.1 Transit-Gym
 T RANSIT-G YM [14] is a SUMO-based general-purpose transit simulator carefully calibrated for
the city of Chattanooga. This has been achieved by careful calibration of the underlying model.
Note that for the simulator to remain viable, it is crucial to keep the physical transit network of
the city and the simulated transit network in sync. The geometric design of roads evolves as
new roads, and additional lanes are built on existing transportation infrastructure. This adds a
layer of complexity to developing precise simulation environments. It is common for developers
and researchers to procure transportation data, and network files from existing platforms, such as
OpenStreetMap (OSM) [15]. The data available on these platforms is traditionally crowdsourced,
and in many cases, it is not up to date with the actual transportation infrastructure. Therefore, we
have developed and integrated specialized procedures to perform a stochastic check against the
designed network and real traffic data collected from the city and then fix any discrepancies in
the network. This process ensures that road geometries and map files are continuously updated,
providing an accurate environment for testing and validation.
  Fig. 7 presents the framework of T RANSIT-G YM. SUMO [16] is used to conduct microscopic
traffic simulations, wherein vehicles, public transport, and persons are modeled explicitly. A sce-
nario description language that comes with the simulator provides the ability to customize the
transit simulation configurations. The simulation gym is equipped with microscopic energy pre-
diction models developed by the team [17, 18, 19] previously. They are used to estimate the energy

 Page 10

                               Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025

 Figure 7: Framework of simulation platform.

 Figure 8: Passenger occupancy of each bus along the bus stops by trips across 24 hours.

consumption for each vehicle trip as simulated through the SUMO network.
  Overall, the simulation engine is capable of providing road-based traffic measurement output,
including the macroscopic values such as the mean speed, the mean density, the mean occupancy
of road edge during specified time intervals. For each bus stop, we output the simulated schedule:
time of arrival and departure, stopping place, and the number of persons that boarded and got
off from the bus. The passenger itineraries are configurable and are simulated based on input
demand models provided by the local transportation planning office. For each transit vehicle,
we also provide current speed and acceleration. Finally, we also provide the estimated energy
consumption, calculated after the simulation using the energy estimation models.
  Examples of the output analysis visualizations are as follows. Fig. 8 shows the passenger oc-
cupancy of each bus along with its stops by trips for a whole day. Fig. 9a shows the maximum
passenger occupancy of each bus along with the bus stops by routes. The bar within each box
represents the median of the maximum passenger occupancy of different buses on each route and

the two sides of box correspond to the first and the third quartile of the data for each route. To
investigate the occupancy status during different times in a day, the distributions of bus occupancy

ferent routes. Fig. 10b presents the comparison of the energy consumption rate of buses among
different trip assignment scenarios. It shows that using all-electric vehicles is the best strategy in
terms of energy consumption.

2.2.2 E-Transit Bench: Extending the Transit-Gym with Electric Grid Simulation
  The integrated simulation is designed to make the individual underlying components work in
unison, namely, the power grid simulation (using GridLAB-D) and the vehicle transit simulation
(using SUMO). The information exchange that goes on between these simulators needs to be care-
fully handled as it is time-sensitive. This information exchange is performed with the use of the
Hierarchical Engine for Large-scale Infrastructure Co-Simulation (HELICS) [20], an open-source
co-simulation framework. It is the Co-simulator coordinator, responsible for time synchronization,
and also acts as an agent to interact with the grid models in GridLAB-D and the Python feder-
ate. There are dedicated computations modules programmed in Python to perform computations
that interact with the Python federate, that utilize the various parameters or information from the
interface. All of these components work in tandem and run the entire simulation platform syn-
chronously.
  The work is described in Fig. 11. GridLAB-D subscribes to the state of charge (SoC) values from

 Page 13

                                                      Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025
                    288                                                                 80                                           200                                       40

 30

 285 50 20

 283 30
 282 20 50 0
 281 10 Real Loss Reactive Losses CH#1 CH#2 CH#3 CH#4 CH#5
 280 0 0
 Charging instances
                          0     5       10       15     20      25                           0     5     10       15   20       25
 Time (hrs) Time (hrs) Usage (non-peak) Usage (peak)

 (a) (b) (c)

Figure 13: Results from the transit simulation for a day in January in Chattanooga. (a) Node Voltage at the charger
location. (b) Total Feeder Loss. (c) Usage charge breakdown in 5 charging instances.

the transit simulator when the EV arrives at the charging station to get charged. This translates to
the grid as an additional load that follows a charging profile, predefined for the EV battery and
the charger at the charging station. The charger profile is also season-dependent (it uses the city
weather profile) to ensure the charging time and the impact on the grid behavior are realistic.
  The simulation goes on for a specified time duration. During this time, it can monitor the move-
ment of all the individual vehicles (electric buses) as well as the state of charge (SoC) of the
vehicles. The important checks that go on in the simulation are: (i) checking the SoC of electric
buses and how much it has depleted, and (ii) checking for any changes in the grid load. The SOC
of the bus drops as it moves around in its set route. When the SoC is below a critical level, the
bus needs to charge soon to replenish its battery, or else, it may run out of charge and stop in its
tracks. We want to avoid that scenario and direct the bus to a charger as soon as possible. As soon
as the bus moves on to the charging location and charging begins, its movements are stopped and
the power consumption is added to the grid, in the grid simulation. The grid load is then measured
throughout the period of charging the bus and can be done for multiple charging buses at a time.
This is important as it provides an understanding of how much power is drawn from the grids at
the charging stations and more importantly, how much the grid is affected by this usage and its
effects on the surrounding neighborhoods.
  Once the bus reaches the desired charge level, it is disconnected from the grid. The power draw
at that charger is reduced to zero. The transit sim then resumes the movement of the bus along its
planned route for the rest of the duration. This cycle can go on whenever the buses are low on a
battery charge or are pre-planned to charge. Data about all the required parameters (like SoC, bus
movement, weather, trips completed, and so on) can be collected and stored during this period.
  The simulation ends when it has run for the desired duration. The Python federate gets the
message from both the grid simulator and the transit simulator that their execution has stopped.
Subsequently, the federate performs any necessary post-processing on the collected data (like grid
analysis, and bus efficiency). After these are performed, the program terminates, marking the end
of the integrated simulation cycle.
  We now describe the analysis of a day from January 11, 2022 as simulated on the E-transit
bench. The SoCs for the electric vehicles is then generated (Fig. 12a) and made available to the
grid simulator for analysis of the GIS. The average speed of the electric buses is shown in Fig. 12b.
During the simulation, we constantly check the SOC of the vehicles and then estimate when the
vehicle is ready for charging (once it finishes its trip in the transit simulation). Figs. 13a and 13b
demonstrate the impact of charging during the peak and off-peak load times. The voltage dips
during charging due to an increase in the load and since the charger is located at the end of the
feeder the loss increase in the system during the charging times can be observed clearly.

 Page 14

                                       Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025

  A grid impact score (GIS) is determined for the given scenario and location of the charger. This
GIS depends on various parameters like the location of the charger, seasonal impact on the charging
profile. The GIS, shown in Fig. 12c, is relatively higher during charging (compared to the instant
just before charging) as there is a significant drop in voltage and an increase in the system losses.
The GIS is a normalized value and if it is greater than 1, it indicates a violation of the grid operating
limits. The GIS will be used in the future work on the project to coordinate the charging events to
minimize the impact to the grid. The usage charge breakdown is shown in Fig. 13c.
2.3 Improving the Occupancy Estimation Algorithm
  Accurately estimating the maximum occupancy of each scheduled transit-vehicle trip is crucial
to understanding the demand for transit in an area. This data is useful for generative models
that we use in non-myopic optimization as well as real-time adjustments to headways and transit
schedule—decisions that impact the overall efficiency of the transit operations. Our team has
worked on developing two approaches for solving this problem. We describe them below.
2.3.1 Using Neural Network Architectures
  The first approach is a statistical procedure where we set up a deep neural network architecture to
learn the underlying distribution as a function of several covariates, including the weather and the
region of operation. These neural network models form the core of the generative models discussed
in our MC-VRP work (Section 2.1.3). Further, we designed a neural-architecture and feature
search algorithm that fine-tunes the architecture’s hyper-parameters and predictor variables. [21].
The hyper-parameter and feature-set optimization is based on a randomized local search, which
minimizes both prediction error and the complexity of the model. Minimizing the complexity of
the model is crucial since it reduces computational cost and time during both training and inference.
 Best Architecture

 (a) (b)

Figure 14: (a) Neural architecture search with score based on model complexity and the average RMSE over all tasks.
(b) Comparison between architectures that were found by generic (yellow ) and task-specific searches (blue ) based
on NAS score for each specific task.

 Fig. 14a shows the first 900 scores of the accepted architectures of a generic search. After
trying around 600 different architectures, the search reaches the best architecture based on average
RMSEs on the validation sets and model complexity. Fig. 14b shows the score after training for
a specific transit route with both generic and route-specific architectures. There is a clear gap
between the two losses, which indicates that the architecture and feature set found by the route-
specific search outperforms the ones found when optimizing for all route. So, prediction error and
model complexity can be reduced by optimizing the hyper-parameters for a specific route, which
is made possible by our novel search algorithm.

(from 0% to 100%) about the presence of a passenger; we reduce false-positive errors by filtering all detections below
80% confidence.

Feature Search for Predicting the Ridership of Public Transportation Routes”. In: 8th IEEE Inter-
national Conference on Smart Computing (SMARTCOMP). June 2022.
2.3.2 Using Computer Vision
  The second approach is to improve vehicle-load information accuracy and reliability by lever-
aging data streams from existing on-board video cameras on transit vehicles. Specifically, the
goal is to study the feasibility of applying state-of-the-art machine-learning based computer-vision
models to videos recorded on transit vehicles in order to estimate ridership, and to develop proof-
of-concept implementations.
 Table 4: Results with a model based on Faster R-CNN [22], a state-of-the-art object-detection architecture.

 10-fold cross validation Precision Recall Mean absolute percentage error
 Average over folds 97% 93% 8%

  In Phase I of the project, we accomplished the following key results in the area of computer-
vision based ridership estimation. We collected two diverse datasets (one low-resolution video
dataset and one high-resolution image dataset, covering both daytime and nighttime as well as
crowded and non-crowded settings) from transit vehicles operated by the public transit agency of
Chattanooga, TN. We developed formal guidelines for annotating videos and images with ground-
truth labels for on-board passengers, considering the unique requirements and challenges of our
application. We manually annotated the video dataset by attaching a ground-truth label to every
passenger on every frame, which enables using the video datasets to train and evaluate computer-
vision models. We trained multiple state-of-the-art computer-vision models to detect passengers
using our own video dataset as well as public computer-vision datasets. We created an ensemble-
averaging based committee machine, which combines the outputs of these computer-vision models

 Page 16

                               Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025

 Figure 16: Integrated System Architecture of the designed system

to provide more accurate predictions than the individual models. Finally, we rigorously evaluated
the computer-vision models, considering the metrics of mean absolute percentage error, recall, and
precision. Fig. 15 shows sample images from the training and test sets. Table 4 shows the results
from a dataset of 600 images. Our mean absolute percentage error achieved on this set was 8%.
2.4 Application Programming Interfaces
  One of the last tasks of Phase I was the design of a paratransit software application that can be de-
ployed at CARTA. The architecture we chose is a REST based micro-services architecture. Fig. 16
describes the complete system architecture that the team has developed for Phase I. It includes
online data streaming and storage capabilities, including the integration of the vehicle telemetry
storage system that the team developed in the prior DOE project “High-Dimensional Data-Driven
Energy Optimization for Multi-Modal Transit Agencies.” The architecture also includes the para-
transit application that is described next. This application integrates the scheduling algorithms
described earlier in the report.
2.4.1 Paratransit Web Application
  The paratransit application provides web-based paratransit operations services to transit agen-
cies. The application aims to improve the efficiency and consistency of reservation/bookings pro-
cess for rides, the ability to schedule and modify the ordering and assignment of trips to the opti-
mal vehicles and trip routes, and the ability to have insights into metrics and tools of historical and
planned vehicle runs. Further, the application makes use of automatically optimizing all scheduled
routes for a day given a fleet of vehicles, but also gives management the ability to make ad-hoc
changes to a vehicles manifest, remove or add vehicles to the fleet, quickly modify reservations,
and view real-time shuttle locations with status of their planned trips.
  The application’s services are built upon a strong data management platform. Application ser-
vices utilize data including rider information and historical trips, active fleet vehicles and their
model’s ambulatory and wheelchair capacities, versioned daily Timetables, which allow easily
edited client trip information, reordering and reassignment of trips throughout the vehicle fleet,
and editable vehicle manifests containing the details of each trip that will be serviced by a particu-
lar vehicle on a given day. The application was developed utilizing the Dash Plotly user interface

 Page 17

                               Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025

 Figure 17: Applications Hub of the Paratransit Web Application Developed by the Team in Phase I

and Flask micro web framework, both in Python 3.8. Dash is written on top of Plotly.js and Re-
act.js. The backend is developed using a Mongo database.
  These services, described in detail below, were designed to simplify and automate the operations
workflow, reduce data entry errors, and provide more insight into trends and potential cost savings
initiatives. The services also allow for the ability to make ad-hoc changes, review summary metrics
for vehicles, vehicle routes, and daily timetables.

 • Bookings Service: The bookings service allows the creation and saving of rider bookings
 to a centralized database with interactive mapping of addresses. This service allows quick
 searching of a database of existing riders with insight into their information and past trips.
 These rider’s information can be updated quickly, or new riders can also be added. The book-
 ings process also utilizes interactive mapping of pickup and dropoff locations so operators
 can ensure their address meets expectations. A routing algorithm is utilized to provide initial
 draft route distances and durations. Next, a recommendation engine provides an interactive
 scoring mechanism that shows the operator timeframes that may result in improved fuel effi-
 ciency compared to the initial requested pickup time. Finally, a summarize view is provided
 with all client and trip details along with interactive maps before saving trip to database for
 scheduling.
 • TimeTable Service: The timetables service allows the creation, review, and modification of
 versioned Timetables. A timetable consists of a set of vehicle runs, which in turn consist of
 a set of requested trips. Each calendar day maintains only one ‘Current’ timetable used for
 dispatch. However, this application keeps a versioned history of timetables identified by a
 version number and timestamp showing the last time it was saved. Operators can work off the
 ‘current’ version or choose an older version with which to start their review or modification.
 Operators are allowed to reorder trips within a vehicle, reassign trips from one vehicle to

 Page 18

                               Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025

 another, cancel/delete trips, modify existing trips, or place trips as temporarily unassigned.
 These unassigned trips can later be assigned to an existing vehicle, or a new vehicle run can
 be created. Batch operations such as the un-assignment of all trips from a vehicle (e.g., in
 the case of a vehicle break-down) can be done.
 • Manager Review Service: The manager review service allows a higher-level review of timeta-
 bles. Summary metrics of the current (or past) timetables are provide, and can be produced
 at the vehicle level, as well. Metrics include total trips assigned to the timetable or vehicle
 run, total distinct riders, first pickup time of the day, and last dropoff time of the day. Man-
 agers can also review changes. For instance, if a client or trip was changed after the ‘current’
 timetable was created, the timetable and associated trip(s) are flagged for review. Changes
 could include a change in pickup/dropoff address or time, or a change in notes specific to the
 client’s requirements, or the fact that a rider might utilize a wheelchair for the first time.
 • Logistics Service: This service provides the ability to make high-level changes to opera-
 tions such as the adding of new vehicles/vehicle manifests or the deletion/removal of vehi-
 cles/vehicle manifests (e.g., if a vehicle was temporarily out of service).
 • Real-time Service [under construction]: The real-time service allows a review of real-time
 locations of paratransit shuttles. The map of the shuttles not only shows locations but show
 which trips have been completed but which trips are in-process or upcoming. It also helps
 enter the real-time same day bookings into the system and helps the driver’s know their
 schedule and plans.

 Figure 18: AI Optimization Engine Framework for Data Integration with Railigent.

Data Collection
 Siemens Technology has primarily focused on exploring the Railigent platform services and in-
terfaces and mapping them to the requirements of the project. The Railigent platform consists
of multiple data ingestion techniques including manual and automatic S3 data ingest, ETL rela-
tional import tool and real-time data processing. It also supports graph databases such as Amazon
Neptune and Neo4j, a key requirement of the project for representing the origin-destination (OD)

 Page 19

                               Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025

matrices and dependency graphs. Further, the platform provides APIs and blueprint templates
to speed up the development work. Transit Operations Insights (TOI) of Railigent is a power-
ful visualization tool to render the insights for the operation of vehicle fleets. We have gauged
the cloud-based Railigent platform and its TOI application for the specific needs of agencies with
mixed-vehicle vehicles. Specifically, we identified the interfaces that we are going to utilize and
build upon to import CARTA datasets to Railigent platform and set up the infrastructure to execute
JSON schema transformation. The next steps will involve defining and implementing the interfaces
and schema transformation APIs to import the datasets. Figure 18 shows the entire framework with
the integration with the Railigent platform.
  The complete API documentation is included in the appendix (Appendix A).

remaining respondents came from CARTA bus riders (10%) and the CARTA email list (12%). The
majority of the respondents in the study used the bus (47%) as their primary form of transportation,
while roughly 38% of respondents indicated they used other modes of transportation to get around
(ie., personal vehicle, bike, ride-sharing, or bike-sharing. The bus rider sample were from our
efforts with the Chattanooga Housing Authority, CARTA bus riders, the CARTA email list, and
target organizations. Initial survey of our CARTA GO microtransit application began in May,
2023, to create baseline data for future pilot operations. This included personal on-board surveys
with passengers and drivers, and development of a directed survey plan in the following quarter.
The results of the initial focus groups and surveys are shown in Fig. 19.

3 Performance Analysis and Go/No Go Milestone
 To analyze the performance of our algorithms in saving energy while maintaining 100 percent
service rate, we compared the algorithms discussed in Section 2.1 for the week of Monday April
12, 2021. Recall that the MC-VRP [5] (Section 2.1.3) is a fully online, non-myopic approach to
solve the dynamic vehicle routing problem (DVRP) for paratransit services. For this comparison
we used the budget-based heuristic which aims to maximize the time a vehicle has no passengers
on board to provide slack for future requests. The heuristic is used to prune the search space and
select a finite number of promising actions which are then evaluated with Monte Carlo tree search.
The approach is non-myopic in that it considers future states of the system at each time epoch
without relying on models trained offline.
 We used the vehicle capacity of 9 passengers and required that all passengers will picked up
within 15 minutes of their requested pickup time. Additionally, when a customer requests a trip we
provide an estimated dropoff time which is the sum of the requested pickup time and the minimum
travel time to the dropoff location. The late time window is 15 minutes after the estimated dropoff
time which is again, in accordance with settings used by CARTA. Our results (Fig. 20) show that
our MC-VRP algorithm used 42 vehicle trips. The actual CARTA real-world assignment used 96
vehicle trips. The Google OR Tools used 141 vehicle trips. The online bookings algorithm with
a 4 hour tight window used 71 vehicle runs. The online bookings algorithm with a 2 hour tight
window used 64 vehicle runs. This improvement is greater than 5% that was indicated in our
milestone.
 Recall that in earlier work [1], we had showed that our approach of fixed line scheduling where
we choose the best vehicle class for each trip can save $399 of Energy Cost daily, which is approx-
imately 5% of daily cost. In the future work, we are working on integrated scheduling, which we
believe can lead to even more energy savings.

Our tasks and the current progress in Phase III are summarized below.
 1. Design experiments to validate the feasibility of our approach on an actual field test.
 2. Develop the field tests with the fully integrated system.
 3. Deploy a complete end-to-end pilot within CARTA’s real world workflow, that would allow
 us to evaluate the performance improvement provided by our AI Optimization Engine.
 The tasks and their status are shown in Table 5.
4.1 Field Test Preparation
 For the final phase of the project, we aimed at testing the platform in a real-world setting. This
deployment was essential not only for validating the software’s capabilities but also for optimizing
transit operations within the Chattanooga community through effective data-driven decisions. In
collaboration with the Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority (CARTA), we suc-
cessfully launched the platform into their existing infrastructure as part of a pilot, focusing on
enhancing paratransit and microtransit services.
4.1.1 Algorithm Evaluation and Pre-Deployment
 We run experiments for all solvers with real-world paratransit data from CARTA from August
13th, 2023. The paratransit fleet consisted of 15 vehicles available with schedules staggered be-
tween morning and afternoon shifts according to CARTA’s driver and vehicle availability and a
total of 125 passengers. Each request in the dataset consisted of a geo-coordinate (longitude, lat-
itude) for the pick-up location and drop-off location, as well as the requested pickup time. It also
contained the capacity type for each request, with the option of ambulatory or wheelchair.
 In addition to the set of requests, we also use CARTA’s scheduled manifest and driver schedules
for August 13th, 2023, where a manifest refers to an ordered list of locations that a vehicle will
visit. Each entry in the manifest is marked with an action, pick-up or drop-off, and estimated arrival
time. The manifest and driver schedules allow us to calculate CARTA’s metrics for comparison
against our solvers. CARTA uses a black-box software product to generate the manifests. We
instantiate constraints for the VRP by using the same real-world constraint that CARTA follows:
1) the time window, i.e., the amount of time before he requested pickup time that a request can be
picked up, is set to 15 minutes; 2) The vehicle capacity is set to maximum 8 ambulatory passengers
and 2 wheelchair passengers (based on CARTA’s paratransit vans); 3) the dwell time, i.e., the
estimated duration that a vehicle will stay at a location to pick-up/drop-off a passenger is set to 5
minutes; and 4) any trip request that can not be serviced within the time windows is dropped, i.e.,
not considered.
4.1.2 Offline Solver Evaluation
 Prior to extensive deployment, the algorithmic approaches were rigorously validated both in
simulation using real-world paratransit data from CARTA and two small pilots [23]. Experi-
ments demonstrated that the proposed offline solver significantly improved efficiency compared
to CARTA’s existing black-box software. The solver reduced Vehicle Miles Traveled (VMT) per
Passenger Miles Traveled (VMT/PMT) by 21.3%, achieved an 85% shared rate (meaning more
than one passenger on board for a given time), and eliminated time window violations present in
CARTA’s original manifest. Similarly, the online solver showed improved performance even when
accommodating real-time requests, particularly in scenarios resembling CARTA’s typical operating
conditions (e.g., a 90-10 split between day-ahead and real-time requests). This validation provided
a strong foundation for subsequent pilot deployments of similar on-demand transit technologies.

 Page 23

                                 Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025

Figure 21: Solver Metrics recorded for paratransit data on compared with PTA’s real schedule. VMT: Vehicle Miles
Travelled, VDM: Vehicle Detour Miles, VMT/PMT: Vehicle Miles Travelled to Passenger Miles Travelled, Shared
Rate: percentage of trip requests that shared their trip with another passenger, Violations: number of time window
constraint violations. We observe that the proposed offline solver serves all passengers without any violations, with
significantly fewer detour miles and a significantly higher shared rate, thereby reducing the total number of miles the
vehicles have to drive.

4.1.3 Online Solver Evaluation
  To evaluate the online solver, we simulate real-time requests from the real-world data collected
from CARTA. We randomly split the set of requests into two parts and treat one part as the set
of day-ahead requests and another as the set of real-time requests. Our experiment design mimics
CARTA’s operational constraints, where a set of requests are available a day in advance, and at
times, real-time requests must be accommodated while serving the day-ahead requests. For ex-
ample, a 70-30 split denotes that 70% of requests are available a day in advance (to be solved by
the offline solver), and 30% of requests arrive in real-time as same-day requests. In this case, our
online solver is called for each real-time request as they arrive. The request arrival time, defined
as the time before the requested pickup time that the request is available to the system, is set to 30
minutes.
  We present the results of the online solver in Fig. 21. We observe that even when 70% of the
requests are known in advance, the proposed approach outperforms CARTA’s approach even if it
knows all requests in advance. The performance gain is captured in lower VMT/PMT ratios, lower
VMT, lower VDM, and a higher shared rate. We also observe that if a significant proportion of
the trip requests are made in real-time (e.g., more than 40% of requests), then the proposed online
solver fails to accommodate all requests while maintaining all paratransit constraints; e.g., in such
a situation, 115 out of 125 passengers are served. In practice, the current operating conditions of
CARTA resemble a 90-10 split, which the online solver can easily accommodate.
4.1.4 Improvement to the Operational Backbone
  The pilot’s operational backbone is an overall improvement of the initial paratransit web appli-
cation that was created during the initial phases of the project. The new application is built upon
three custom-developed and interconnected applications [24] we called “SmartTransit.AI”:

 • Dispatcher App ( Fig. 22): A cloud-hosted web application that enabled CARTA personnel to
 book rides for customers, track real-time operations, generate reports, and manage schedule
 modifications.

 • Driver App ( Fig. 24 (left)): Installed on iPads within the service vehicle, this application pro-
 vided real-time schedule information to drivers and transmitted pickup/drop-off data, along
 with location details.

 • Client App ( Fig. 24 (right)): A cloud-hosted web application, optimized for mobile viewing,

 Page 24

                                Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025

Figure 22: Supervisors can edit the schedule and override the machine-generated schedule through dispatcher web
application

 that allowed customers to sign up for the service, plan and book trips, and monitor their trip
 progress. However, practical issues such as low cell phone and internet access among users
 meant the client app was primarily utilized by customer service for trip planning rather than
 direct customer usage.
 A key innovation is the modular and reusable software design, which allows new algorithms
to be incorporated by simply changing a configuration file, as solvers are accessible via a REST
API. The software, shown in Fig. 23) is designed with the domain knowledge of transit experts in
mind. Thus, the focus on integrating human-in-the-loop, enabling human overriding of machine-
generated schedules. Operators can drag and drop scheduled trips, with the system providing im-
mediate feedback on potential constraint violations, and changes are communicated to drivers in
real-time. The system also offers real-time operations monitoring via the web application, display-
ing vehicle locations and route status, and alerting the team to violations, no-shows, and anticipated
delays.
4.2 Initial Pilot Test
 We selected August 3, 2023, and August 10, 2023, for the pilot. We generated new manifests
based on the proposed approach, which were actually deployed by CARTA. To compare the ef-
ficacy of the proposed approach, CARTA also generated manifests using their existing black-box
approach. We exported the trip requests, driver schedules, vehicles, and scheduled manifests from
CARTA’s existing system. On both days, there were 15 vehicles available, with schedules stag-
gered between morning and afternoon shifts according to CARTA’s driver and vehicle availability.
There were a total of 159 passengers and 129 passengers on August 3rd and 10th, respectively.
 For the deployment, CARTA followed strict time window constraints for two types of passenger
requests. CARTA mandated that pickup-constrained requests must be picked up within a 15-minute
window before or after the requested pickup time, and the passenger must be dropped off within an
hour of the requested pickup time at their destination. Dropoff-constrained requests represent situ-
ations where a passenger must be dropped off before the requested dropoff time and must be picked
up no earlier than one hour before the appointment (e.g., these constraints often represent medi-
cal appointments in CARTA’s data). Additionally, each vehicle had two capacity constraints—no
more than 8 ambulatory passengers and 2 wheelchair passengers could be on a vehicle at a given

 Page 25

                                Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025

Figure 23: A screenshot of the real-time operations view in the operations management page of the dispatcher app
while a driver served route 15

time.
4.2.1 Initial Pilot Evaluation
  We present the key metrics related to the performance of CARTA’s original schedule compared
to the schedule generated by the offline VRP solver in Fig. 25. As shown, the proposed approach
reduced VMT by 356 miles on August 3, 2023, and by 236 miles on August 10, 2023. There was
a 24% and 17% improvement in VMT/PMT over CARTA’s initial paratransit schedule for August
3, 2023, and August 10, 2023, respectively. The efficiency gain correlates with the finding that our
implementation had a much higher shared Rate, which is the percentage of passengers who shared
their trip with at least one other passenger compared to CARTA’s schedule.
4.3 Clifton Hills Deployment
  The culmination of the project is the Clifton Hills microtransit pilot program, an initiative by
CARTA to explore the efficacy of flexible, on-demand transit solutions in meeting community
mobility needs. Conducted over 27 service days in June and July 2024. During its operational
hours, from 9 am to 3 pm, a dedicated vehicle, driver, and booking agent were deployed to serve
the community. The pilot served as a practical demonstration and evaluation of novel technol-
ogy solutions for on-demand transit, building upon extensive research in paratransit optimization
and vehicle routing problems. The program utilized a custom-developed system architecture com-
prising dispatcher, driver, and client applications, aiming to provide energy-efficient and equitable
transit access in areas typically underserved by traditional fixed-route services. Despite facing
operational and technological hurdles, the pilot achieved significant community engagement and
provided invaluable insights, confirming the potential of microtransit as a feeder system for high-
capacity public transport.
  The insights gained from this pilot will be instrumental in refining and expanding microtransit
services to better serve the community. Our long-term vision is to provide on-demand microtransit
services that act as feeders for high-capacity, fixed-route transit services. Implementing this vision
will enable agencies to provide energy-efficient and equitable transit access in areas with low pop-

 Page 26

                                Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025

 Figure 24: Different application screens for (left) drivers, (middle and right) client

Figure 25: Pilot Metrics recorded for system testing for both deployments. VMT: Vehicle Miles Travelled, VDM:
Vehicle Detour Miles, VMT/PMT: Vehicle Miles Travelled to Passenger Miles Travelled, Shared Rate: percentage of
trip requests that shared their trip with another passenger. We observe that the proposed offline solver serves all
passengers without any violations, with significantly fewer detour miles and a significantly higher shared rate,
thereby reducing the total number of miles the vehicles have to drive.

ulation density, which are often underserved by existing transit solutions, by combining the energy
efficiency of high-capacity transit with the flexibility of microtransit. The Clifton Hills microtransit
pilot served to demonstrate and evaluate our novel technology solutions for on-demand transit.
 The pilot focused on the Clifton Hills community, shown in Section 4.3, with the primary opera-
tions hub located at the Metropolitan Ministries (MetMin). MetMin, a local impact center offering
services related to housing and food insecurity and medical needs, served as a key location for
on-site ride bookings and as a bus station. While most trips were booked in person at MetMin,
requests were also accommodated via phone from other locations.
4.3.1 Ridership Statistics
 Throughout the service period, the Clifton Hills pilot successfully completed 55 public requests,
in addition to numerous simulated and internal requests. In total, 68 rides were completed, with
round trips counted as two separate rides. This averaged approximately 2.5 rides per service day.
Of the 45 individuals who registered for the service, 24 ultimately utilized it by taking at least one
ride. On average, customers took 2.8 rides each, with 12 repeat customers indicating a degree of
satisfaction and utility. The service experienced a peak of six rides in a single day, although there
were seven days with no ridership. The installation of a bike rack two weeks into the pilot, on June

 Page 27

                                Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025

Figure 26: (a) The area covered by the Clifton Hills project. (b) A CARTA driver using the driver application to
identify the next stop on their trip.

18th, was noted as a potential enhancement to the service’s appeal.
4.3.2 Common Destinations
 The primary operations hub was MetMin, a local impact center that offers services to individuals
facing housing and food insecurity, medical needs, and more. MetMin’s facilities were utilized for
on-site booking and as a station for the bus. Most trips were booked in person at MetMin, although
requests were also taken via phone from other locations.
 The most frequently visited destination was Market Street, which emerged as the most popular
location outside of MetMin, accounting for 18 trips. Market Street was a favorable location for
customers that were transfering to fixed routes. Other common destinations included Workman
Road (an encampment area, outside the service zone, with 13 trips) and East 11th Street (Chatt
Foundation/community kitchen, also outside the service zone, with 12 trips). Additional destina-
tions included doctor’s appointments, Suboxone treatment centers, social security offices, shelters,
and potential job sites, illustrating the diverse needs of the service users.
 Regarding trip origins, 17 rides were not booked on-site at MetMin but were instead arranged
over the phone by CARTA dispatchers or pre-arranged at MetMin. Furthermore, six rides did not
originate or terminate at MetMin, indicating some flexibility in the service’s operational scope.
4.3.3 Operational Obstacles
 As with any innovative initiative, the pilot encountered both practical and technological chal-
lenges.
 • Practical Issues: Initially, the absence of a bike rack led to some potential riders being turned
 away. A recurring challenge involved requests for trips slightly outside the designated ser-
 vice zone, complicating adherence to established boundaries. Furthermore, low cell phone
 and internet access among users limited the direct utilization of the customer app by patrons,
 necessitating its use by customer service as a trip planner.
 • Technological Issues: Technical hurdles included a server going offline due to a power out-

 Page 28

                               Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025

 age, issues with dispatcher feedback for input field validations, difficulties with driver adop-
 tion and understanding of the technology, system instability when all daily requests were
 canceled, and errors related to concurrency protections. Despite these technical impedi-
 ments, the team’s ability to implement quick workarounds and fixes ensured nearly 100%
 service availability.

4.3.4 Community Engagement
 The community engagement component of this project seeks to study the ecology of participa-
tory organizations in Chattanooga and, once identified, to connect with indigenous networks of
residents who are engaged with those organizations. This socio-relational approach to community
engagement will facilitate identifying organizations as well as indigenous networks of residents.
This contrasts with historical approaches to outreach that focus on mass dissemination of surveys
and flyers without regard to existing personal relationships in the community. Our data collec-
tion process is less concerned about transportation per se than about the key issues and struggles
communities are dealing with (i.e., employment opportunities, development of their children with
education and safe activities, care for loved ones like elderly parents, access and affordability of
healthy foods). All these struggles have transportation components to them, but we are looking
first to what struggles are priorities, and only then will we inquire about transportation options
to those priority issues. Another way of thinking about this is that our community engagement
process is not looking for customer preferences, but for resident interests.
 The outreach strategy of our focus groups and interviews is designed to support an increase in
ridership and public utilization of the transit system that is being designed. We are currently in the
first phase of our engagement plan and have started setting up the focus groups. In addition, we
have started aggregating and merging data providing information about census and demographics
variable.
4.3.5 Lessons Learned
 The Clifton Hills microtransit pilot successfully demonstrated the tangible potential of flexible,
on-demand transit solutions in effectively addressing community mobility needs. This deployment,
as a culmination of research into advanced vehicle routing and paratransit optimization, provided
invaluable real-world insights. Despite navigating various practical and technological challenges,
the pilot achieved significant engagement and maintained consistent service availability.
 The lessons learned from this pilot are crucial for refining and expanding future microtransit
services, ensuring they better serve communities in a more energy-efficient and equitable manner.
The collaborative approach, integrating robust algorithmic optimization with crucial human over-
sight, proved key to adapting and maintaining service continuity, thereby paving the way for more
seamless and inclusive urban transportation systems.
5 Conclusion and DOE Impact
  Enhancing public transit competitiveness is especially critical in mid-sized cities typically char-
acterized by relatively low-density land use, widely available parking opportunities, and limited
transit coverage and frequency. This project is developing operational optimization algorithms that
are energy efficient and will support a more significant percentage of the population, improving
their transportation choice through enhanced access to transit. The project provides mechanisms to
improve transit accessibility with a projected increase from 41% to 73% of residents in the planned
study areas while optimizing the energy efficiency of the mixed fleet operations. This will reduce

 Page 29

                               Chattanooga Area Regional Transportation Authority | August 2025

single-occupancy vehicle trips by those residents, significantly decreasing transportation energy
use at the city level.

core/config.py/VEHICLES_COLLECTION_NAME.
 vehicle_model_id (int): see VEHICLE_MODELS
 vehicle_id (int): unique identifier of this vehicle
 active (bool): True if this vehicle is currently active, False if not (for example because its in the
 shop).

Unique keys: vehicle_id

Foreign keys: vehicle_model_id (VEHICLE_MODELS)
  OPERATORS = {'operator_id': <class 'int'>, 'first_name': <class 'str'>, 'last_name': <class
  'str'>, 'phone': <class 'int'>, 'email': <class 'str'>, 'last_modified': 'datetime_obj'}
Description: the operators data schema in Mongo. Operators are drivers to drive the care-a-vans. For
collection name in Mongo see core/config.py/OPERATORS_COLLECTION_NAME. TODO need to add
operators to development Mongo database.
 operator_id (int): unique identifier of this operator
 first_name (str): first name of the operator
 last_name (str): last name of the operator
 phone (int): phone number of the operator (for example: 1231231234)
 email (str): email address of the operator (for example: john@gmail.com)

Unique keys: operator_id
Foreign keys: None
  DRIVER_RUNS = {'run_id': <class 'int'>, 'start_time': <class 'str'>, 'end_time': <class
  'str'>, 'activation_date': <class 'str'>, 'inactivation_date': <class 'str'>,
  'vehicle_model_id': <class 'int'>, 'dow': <class 'int'>, 'last_modified': 'datetime_obj'}
Description: the driver runs model schema in Mongo. Driver runs are set blocks of time a vehicle (and a
driver to drive the vehicle) are available on a given day. For collection name in Mongo see
core/config.py/DRIVER_RUNS_COLLECTION_NAME.
 run_id (int): unique identifier of this run.
 start_time (str): time of day this run starts in isoformat (for example: "05:00:00" is 5:00AM,
     "14:00:00" is 2:00PM), EST.
 end_time (str): time of day this run ends in isoformat (for example: "05:00:00" is 5:00AM,
     "14:00:00" is 2:00PM), EST.
 activation_date (str): first date this vehicle run is active in isoformat (for example: "2021-01-01")
 inactivation_date (str): the last date this vehicle run is active in isoformat (for example: "2021-01-
 01")
 vehicle_model_id (int): see VEHICLE_MODELS
 dow (int): day of the week, 0 = Monday.... 6= Sunday

Unique keys: run_id
Foreign keys: vehicle_model_id (VEHICLE_MODELS)
  TIMETABLES = {'date': <class 'str'>, 'state': <class 'str'>, 'timetable_version': <class
  'int'>, 'last_modified': 'datetime_obj'}

timetables are unique by date+timetable_version.
 date (str): date in isoformat (for example: "2021-01-01")
 state (str): active or inactive. There can only be one active planned schedule in a day.
 timetable_version (int): identifier of this timetable.

 MANIFESTS = {'run_id': <class 'int'>, 'timetable_version': <class 'int'>, 'booking_id':
 <class 'int'>, 'order': <class 'int'>, 'early_arrival_dt': 'datetime_obj', 'late_depart_dt':
 'datetime_obj', 'date': <class 'str'>, 'state': <class 'str'>, 'operator_id': <class 'int'>,
 'action': <class 'str'>, 'last_modified': 'datetime_obj'}
Description: the manifests model schema in Mongo. A single manifest is an ordered set of locations
starting at the depot and ending at the depot for a corresponding driver run on a specific date. The
current manifest for a driver run on a date is the manifest with the highest timetable_version. A
timetable is a collection of manifests on a given date with the same timetable_version. A unique
manifest is governed by run_id + date + timetable_version.
 run_id (int): see DRIVER_RUNS
 timetable_version (int): identifier of this timetable.
 booking_id (int): see REQUESTS
 order (int): the order of this location in the unique manifest.
 early_arrival_dt ("datetime_obj"): the datetime at which the vehicle is scheduled to arrive at this
 location.
 late_depart_dt ("datetime_obj"): if the vehicle leaves this location later than late_depart_dt, it is
 running behind and the remaining locations can be affected.
 date (str): date in isoformat (for example: "2021:01:01")
 state (str): either "completed", "in-transit", "planned"
 operator_id (int): see OPERATORS
 action (str): either "depot_start", "depot_end", "pickup" or "dropoff"

Unique key: run_id + date + timetable_version
Foreign keys: run_id (DRIVER_RUNS), operator_id (OPERATORS), booking_id (REQUESTS)
  WHEELCHAIRS = {'wc_type': <class 'int'>, 'name': <class 'str'>, 'wc': <class 'int'>, 'am':
  <class 'int'>, 'manufacturer': <class 'str'>, 'year': <class 'int'>, 'width_inches': <class
  'float'>, 'length_inches': <class 'float'>, 'last_modified': 'datetime_obj'}
Description: the wheel chairs model schema in Mongo. It describes the type of wheelchairs available in
the CARTA system. For collection name in Mongo see
core/config.py/WHEELCHAIR_COLLECTION_NAME. TODO: need to add this to the mongo dev
database.
 wc_type (int): unique identifier of this wheelchair type

 name (str): the name of this wheelchair (for example, "standard wheelchair")
 wc (int): how many WC seats this wheelchair takes up (used in optimization)
 am (int): how many ambulatory seats this takes up
 manufacturer (str): text of the manufacturer
 year (int): year of this wheelchair model
 width_inches (float): width of wheelchair
 length_inches (float): length of wheelchair

Unique keys: wc_type
Foreign keys: none
